@@130944554 <h> Cheers to the variety that unlocked the western NSW wheat industry <p> Gunnedah district wheat growers Bruce Beeson , Charlie Beeson and Jim O'Reilly inspecting a crop on Leyburn ( 20 kilometres west of Gunnedah ) in about 1938 . Picture supplied by Beeson family collection <p> Bread is often referred to as the staff of life , and 80 years ago , the wheat-growing industry in Australia took a massive step forward with the release of a variety called Gabo . <h> Subscribe now for unlimited access to all our agricultural news across the nation <p> or signup to continue reading <p> All articles from our website &amp; app <p> The digital version of This Week 's Paper <p> Our entire network <p> Gabo was released by the University of Sydney in 1945 and turned the tables for farmers in the Namoi Valley , who had battled with stem rust on the fertile , but previously high-risk , black soil plains . <p> The release of the high-yielding , rust-resistant variety , alongside new farming technologies such as the disc plough , unlocked the region 's agricultural potential @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the farming community 's confidence . <p> The 80th anniversary of the Gabo wheat variety was celebrated in Gunnedah , with the launch of a book , The Story of Gabo , hosted by the Gunnedah Shire Council and the University of Sydney 's Institute of Agriculture . <p> The book , authored by Dr Lindsey O'Brien , Narrabri , and Dr Peter Sharp , Braidwood , and part of the launch , included a visit to the property Gabo , located east of Gunnedah , owned by Tim and Mandy Gavin . <p> Dr O'Brien said there was no doubt Gabo turned wheat farming around in northern NSW . <p> " It became an important factor in helping expand wheat production , " he said . <p> " If you look at the production figures in the book , there 's only about 30,000 tonnes worth of wheat being delivered in the Gunnedah , Moree and Narrabri shires , pre-1960s . <p> Dr O'Brien said the decision by the Australian government in January 1991 , to suspend ( and later abandon ) the Wool Reserve Price Scheme , played @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the price of wool falling overnight from 700 cents a kilogram to 430c/kg . <p> " Farmers always grew a bit of wheat , but tended to be for sheep food , but now suddenly they had access to a variety that can become their main income earner , " he said , of the Gabo variety 's development . <p> The Story of Gabo includes the accounts of Gunnedah district families , the Beesons and Hathways , whose contributions in time , farming expertise , and land for testing and seed production helped bring Gabo to fruition . <p> Walter Waterhouse began working on his goal of developing stem rust-resistant varieties at Sydney University in 1922 . <p> His first two resistant varieties , Fedweb and Hofed , were named and released in 1938 . <p> But , according to Dr O'Brien , the foundation for his development of these varieties was the support of his former Hawkesbury Ag College classmate , Charles Beeson . <p> Mr Beeson provided land , his time , and that of his farm workers to plant , maintain , make observations and harvest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a second-cross ( F2 ) population of Bobin , a drought-tolerant durum called Gaza and another cross of the Bobin genes , which was showing great potential . <p> After region-wide testing by growers and the Department of Agriculture for yield and internationally for rust resistance , a variety named Gabo was identified in 1945 . <p> Claude Hathaway , on the property Woodleigh , Gunnedah , started the seed production process for Gabo with just 55 grains in 1940 , and , by the end of 1945 , had grown this to 2753 bags ( 225,000kg ) . <p> Gabo 's physiology , a beardless variety with hard grains , was important outside the farm gate . <p> " The bread bakers were over the moon because its hard grain gets more starch during the milling process , which takes up more water when being mixed into a dough to make bread , so the baker gets more loaves per ton of flour , " Dr O'Brien said . <p> " Better still , the consumer benefits because they suddenly get access to good quality bread instead of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wheat varieties that dominated production up until that time . " <p> Another impact of Gabo on the wheat industry was its white grain . Prior to its appearance on the market , Australian wheat growers had mostly red grain varieties at their disposal . <p> Dr O'Brien said a mix of white and red wheats did not present a good visual appearance of the Australian product to international buyers . <p> " A ministerial council just asked show societies , ' could they discourage the growing of red grain wheats ? ' " <p> " Show societies dropped heavy hints to wheat competition entrants , and they said , ' Oh , if you want to put one in , it 's okay , but it wo n't win ' . <p> " Of course , growers like to be a bit competitive . They do n't want to be disqualified straight away , and all of a sudden , we 're a white grain country . <p> " No legislation , no raps over the knuckles for growing them . It was just a voluntary move , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ experience in rural and regional journalism , as well as more than six years as a media officer for several politicians and worked in local government . @@130974803 <p> Australia has closed a critical gap in national security legislation to ensure the government can designate foreign state entities as sponsors of terrorism and criminalise support for them . <p> The Criminal Code Amendment ( State Sponsors of Terrorism ) Bill , passed by the lower house last week following review by the Parliamentary Joint Committee on Intelligence &amp; Security ( PJCIS ) , will be immediately used to list the Iran 's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps ( IRGC ) as a terrorist entity and criminalise support for it . <p> This welcome and necessary evolution in Australia 's counterterrorism architecture also confirms the significance of the PJCIS , whose review surfaced important questions about scope , safeguards and future implementation . We explored this in our submission to the review . <p> Instigation for reform comes from a troubling development : use of Australian criminal proxies by the IRGC to carry out arson attacks on Jewish community targets in Melbourne and Sydney in 2024 . These attacks were not only acts of violence ( and interference ) but deliberate attempts to tear at Australia 's social fabric in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and subsequent Gaza conflict -- a characteristic Australia 's prime minister and director-general of security referred to as ' unique ' . <p> Iran 's role in global terrorism is well-documented and includes supporting Hizballah , Hamas and the Houthis , involvement in a deadly 1994 bombing in Buenos Aires , and more recent plots in Europe and Asia . The IRGC , in particular , has long sponsored and supported terrorists . Yet state entities like the IRGC had previously fallen outside the scope of existing Australian counterterrorism laws . <p> Now , by creating a new Part 5.3A in the Criminal Code , the legislation has established a mechanism for listing foreign state entities as sponsors of terrorism and criminalising associated support activities . This mirrors existing laws for non-state actors but adapts them to the unique challenges posed by state involvement . <p> One of the most significant features is the requirement for a specific connection to Australia . Unlike listing non-state terrorist organisations -- for example Boko Haram -- which can , appropriately , occur without direct connection to Australia , a foreign state entity must @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at Australia . <p> This will assist in ensuring listings are grounded in demonstrable threats to Australian interests , thereby avoiding the main risk of the new laws : politically motivated designations driven by outrage at foreign government actions that , however objectionable , do not actually constitute support for terrorism . <p> Notwithstanding this important restriction , the definition of ' targeted at Australia ' is , again appropriately , broad , encompassing acts outside of Australia that harm Australians or Australian interests . On the one hand this rightly recognises the historical terrorist threat to Australians overseas ( such as in Indonesia in the 2000s ) but it also includes the effect of an action -- such as causing or threatening serious harm to an Australian person ( including a permanent resident ) -- rather than requiring proof of conscious intent ( important given the ordinary meaning of ' targeted ' ) . <p> Another important safeguard against inappropriate use by a future government is the explicit exclusion of conduct associated with ' international armed conflict ' from the definition of a terrorist act . This provision is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ already subject to the laws of armed conflict ( including as rendered in existing Australian law ) . <p> However , the exclusion is not without ambiguity . For example , it does not apply to civil wars , and the increasingly grey nature of conflict internationally could lead to future ambiguity . Inclusion of ' occupation ' within the existing Criminal Code definition offers some clarity , but we recommended further refinement to ensure consistent application . <p> While the bill 's passage is a positive and overdue step , our submission to the PJCIS 's review highlighted areas for enhancement that have not been taken up , including reinforcement of the protections against politicisation . This reflected our concern that designating a foreign state entity as a sponsor of terrorism carries significant diplomatic and strategic consequences . Without clear criteria , there is a risk that such listings could be used for inappropriate , symbolic or performative purposes rather than grounded in genuine national security concerns . <p> The foreign minister is required to approve the home affairs minister 's decision to list a state sponsor of terrorism @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ minister on the criteria for exercising that approval , as there 's currently no such guidance provided . Criteria could include considerations of Australia 's national interest , foreign relations , economic security or defence capabilities , for example . Such guidance would not constrain ministerial discretion ( the criteria would not be exclusive ) but would nonetheless clarify Parliament 's intent and promote consistency . <p> We also cautioned against overextending humanitarian exemptions , which could inadvertently create loopholes for hostile actors . While protecting legitimate humanitarian work is essential , these protections must be carefully calibrated to avoid undermining national security . <p> This legislation represents a timely and necessary evolution in Australia 's national security laws . By addressing the gap around state-sponsored terrorism , it strengthens Australia 's ability to respond to emerging threats . It 's yet another reminder that threats constantly evolve and therefore so must legislation , policy and strategy . <p> It 's also confirmation that our current era involves state actors doing what used to be more limited to non-state actors -- carrying out , for example , cyberattacks and criminal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Governments will need to show a greater willingness to manage diplomatic tensions . That Iran was the trigger for this issue meant those particular tensions mattered less to Australia 's economic interests . Future tests will come in relation to countries of far greater economic importance . <p> This article is based on the authors ' submission to the PJCIS inquiry into the State Sponsorship of Terrorism Bill . <p> Author <p> Chris Taylor is head of ASPI 's Statecraft &amp; Intelligence Policy Centre , John Coyne is director of ASPI 's National Security Programs and Justin Bassi is ASPI 's executive director .

AI can not generally provide large parts of documents and/or is not allowed to . It consumes them like a human seeing the work in a library for free . <p> AI first breaks any input into small pieces called tokens . For text that is a few letters or a full word if the word is common . " Toyota " is common enough to have its own token number , but " Ferrari " is split into " Fer " + " rari , each part with a number " . Images are broken into 16x16 pixel pieces , with numbers for colour , texture , or a visual feature such as an edge . <p> Token relationships are then mapped layer by layer into progressively higher-level representations - first within the same layer , and then across deeper layers . Each layer builds on the statistical patterns learned from earlier examples , till eventually a result is inferred as a probability but without understanding . <p> An image might be announced as , " a yellow toy elephant @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a human would instantly recognise that the image was of a toy elephant partly blocking the view of a model Toyota vehicle on a base labelled " Toyota " . @@131053029 <h> Norway Pauses Wealth Fund 's Ethical Divestments <p> Norway 's parliament has voted to temporarily suspend ethical divestments by its sovereign wealth fund , valued at $2.1 trillion and the world 's largest . The decision , put forward by the minority Labour government , arrives amid growing international scrutiny regarding the fund 's investment ethics . The Norwegian sovereign wealth fund was established to manage Norway 's petroleum revenue , investing globally in stocks , bonds , and real estate . Its primary goal is to safeguard and grow the nation 's wealth for future generations . <p> The suspension follows criticism from the U.S. State Department over the fund 's divestment from Caterpillar due to the use of its equipment by Israeli authorities . Finance Minister Jens Stoltenberg highlighted the need to review the ethical guidelines , initially established in 2004 , stating the world has changed considerably since their adoption . These guidelines prevent the fund from investing in companies involved in serious violations of human rights during war or conflict situations . An independent ethics body typically investigates potential breaches and recommends divestments , with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The review and subsequent pause on divestments are expected to last approximately one year . While the proposal received support from the Conservatives , concerns were raised regarding the speed of the decision-making process . Stoltenberg defended the move as necessary to protect the fund , which finances a significant portion of Norway 's public spending and relies heavily on a small number of companies . He noted the fund 's substantial holdings in major tech companies such as Nvidia , Microsoft , Apple , Amazon , Alphabet , Meta , and Broadcom , suggesting the current guidelines could potentially restrict investments in these global giants . <p> Political tensions surrounding the fund 's investments in Israel , particularly during the recent election campaign , also played a role . Some parties campaigned on divesting from companies involved in " Israel 's illegal warfare in Gaza . " The Socialist Left party criticised the government 's decision , suggesting it was motivated by fear of potential reactions from the United States . Separately , the fund indicated it would vote against Tesla CEO Elon Musk 's proposed compensation package @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ news , information analysis and commentary which is general in nature and not financial or investment advice . @@131058150 <h> Save articles for later <p> New York : When he announced his run for mayor in October 2024 , Zohran Mamdani was a state lawmaker unknown to most New York City residents . <p> Now the 34-year-old democratic socialist has had a stunning political ascent , declaring victory in the Democratic primary in June to now becoming mayor of New York in an emphatic victory confirmed by The Associated Press less than an hour after polls closed . <p> New York City mayoral candidate Zohran Mamdani has run a grassroots campaign tapping into voter dissatisfaction.Credit : Bloomberg <p> Mamdani and his challenger , New York Governor Andrew Cuomo , 67 , offered voters starkly different visions on taxes and policing in a race that has , at times , mirrored the divisions roiling the Democratic Party nationally . <p> The election also became a microcosm of New Yorkers ' views on ongoing conflicts in the Middle East , with Cuomo pledging support for Israel as Mamdani criticised the wars in Gaza and Iran . <p> Cuomo started as the favourite in the race , with his deep experience , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> But Mamdani gained momentum , running a sharp campaign laser-focused on the city 's high cost of living , and winning the backing of two of the country 's foremost progressives : congresswoman Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez and Senator Bernie Sanders . <p> " The surge by Mamdani is something I 've never seen before in New York City politics , " said J.C . Polanco , a political analyst at the University of Mount Saint Vincent , before the result was known . <p> So who is the man who has just been elected mayor of New York ? Here 's a closer look at the one-time rapper , the city 's first Muslim and Indian-American mayor , and its youngest mayor in generations . <p> Advertisement <h> Mamdani 's family life <p> Zohran Mamdani was born in Kampala , Uganda in 1991 , to Indian parents . @@131098775 <h> The Union remembers its dead <p> Fatma Hassona ( left ) and Shireen Abu Akleh are among over 100 journalists who have been killed reporting on the war in Gaza ( Images via Wikimedia Commons ) <p> From Gaza to Yemen , the world 's frontlines have become execution grounds for truth-tellers , writes Dr John Jiggens . <p> SUNDAY 2 NOVEMBER was the United Nations International Day to End Impunity for Crimes Against Journalists . Members and friends of the Media Entertainment and Arts Alliance ( MEAA ) gathered in Brisbane 's Reddacliff Place for a vigil to mourn and remember their colleagues killed on duty , reporting on wars during the past year . <p> Addressing the vigil , Kasun Ubayasiri , the federal co-vice president of MEAA 's National Media Section , said that the Committee to Protect Journalists ( CPJ ) has recorded the death of 122 journalists and media workers around the world this year , killed while reporting in combat zones or on dangerous assignments in the previous twelve months . <p> A staggering 61 of these 122 were Palestinian journalists killed in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ workers were killed in a single day in a targeted Israeli airstrike on a newspaper building in Yemen . Three more were killed in an Israeli airstrike on a compound housing journalists in Lebanon . <p> All up , Israeli forces were responsible for 95 of the 122 deaths in the past year . The war in Sudan claimed another 13 journalists ' lives , the largest number in Africa . Another 14 media deaths were sprinkled around the many other global conflict zones . <p> In April , days after the photojournalist and ten members of her family were killed in an Israeli airstrike , the Western media reported that Fatma had declared that " If I die , I want a loud death " . <p> Mughrabi said : <p> In Western and English language coverage of the Palestinian experience of occupation and apartheid and now genocide , much is lost in translation . <p> There can be no doubting that the two missiles which entered the Hassuna family 's second-floor apartment in Gaza City at about 1 AM on April 16th would have made a deafening noise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Her fellow Gazan , the poet Batul Abu Akhaleen , offered a different translation , " If I must die , I want a resonant death . I want to be neither a newsflash nor a number within a group . I want a death heard by the whole world " . <p> Fatma wanted her death to make an indelible mark to change things . Anyone who has made the death of a loved one the impetus for a public appeal , or a new charity , or a campaign to change the law , knows this sentiment . Please make my heartbreaking loss into something positive . <p> The extraordinary photographs Fatma captured with her camera were not just a record of carnage and death , but also of how humans can still walk and work in a space of the worst of humanity . <p> " I 'm trying to find some life in this world , in this death , " she told the Iranian filmmaker Sepideh Farsi . <p> " Every second you walk in the street , you put your soul on your @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ documentary that Farsi made about her its title . That film screened in Cannes in May . Fatima was killed the day after being invited to the premiere . Festival organisers remarked that this young woman 's life force seemed like a miracle . <p> Mughrabi continued : <p> But what force does her death have ? Fatma did not want to be a number within a group . And yet the answer to this question is the same for her as it is for the scores of journalists . <p> I 've become afraid of the moment when my brother 's blood will reach me , will stain me , Fatma wrote . And she asked , if I die , will all my wishes die with me ? <p> Blood reached Fatima Hassuna in April . But if her death does not resonate , the stain will be ours to bear . <p> After the vigil remembering our colleagues , we wrote our names and placed flowers on a large banner with the names of all the dead journalists displayed . <p> This initiative of the MEAA to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Yemeni newspaper and the extraordinary number of Palestinian journalists killed in Gaza -- often with their families -- indicate that Israel regards journalists covering their wars as legitimate targets . However , I was disappointed to see I was one of the few journalists recording this important event . <p> Israel 's war on Gaza has been the single deadliest conflict for journalists ever . In August 2025 , Al Jazeera estimated that more than 270 Palestinian journalists and media workers had been killed in Israel 's war on Gaza -- a staggering figure . <p> ? ? According to Brown University'sCosts of War project , more journalists have been killed in Gaza since the war began on 7 October 2023 than in all the wars since the U.S. Civil War , including World War I , World War II , the Korean War , Vietnam War , the wars in the former Yugoslavia and the post-9/11 wars in the Middle East combined . <p> Australia 's mainstream media remains largely silent about Israel 's targeting of Palestinian journalists and the extraordinary number of our murdered colleagues . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- was in Australia recently to deliver the Edward Said memorial lecture for the Adelaide Friends of Palestine Association ( AFOPA ) . A date was set for Monday 20 October . @@131172665 <p> If ever there were a time to resurrect science journalism , it is now . This year it 's edited by journalists Zoe Kean and Tegan Taylor and tackles many pressing contemporary subjects in 39 reprinted articles by science journalists from across the country . Topics range from the merely curious to the politically and environmentally pressing . Could borderline personality disorder be a form of PTSD ? As part of her duties , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Vietnam , Aruba , Colombia and India . <h> The complex relationship between beauty pageants and politics <p> Dr Hannah McCann , senior lecturer in cultural studies at the University of Melbourne , said culturally , the " significance of beauty pageants to feminism -- it kind of ca n't be understated " . <p> The women 's liberation movement gained notoriety for its protest of the 1968 Miss America pageant , where protesters paraded sheep and held signs , opposing what they viewed as a symbol of women 's oppression . Protesters also had a ' Freedom trash can ' , which they threw magazines , lipsticks and bras into . <p> " This is where the myth of the feminist bra burning comes from , " McCann said . <p> The protest of the 1968 Miss America pageant was organised by the New York Radical Women group . " <p> The 1996 Miss World pageant in Bengaluru , India , attracted protests from all sides of politics . The Nobel is often awarded to more unentitled , selfless @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out their work with a degree of humility . <p> And indeed , the 2025 recipient , Venezuelan Opposition Leader Maria Corina Machado , was at pains to acknowledge Trump in her acceptance speech . She 's clearly someone who does n't relish hogging the limelight , and thus an even more worthy recipient of the prize . <p> It makes sense when you consider how many past winners have been people most of us had never heard of before . Of course , we should reward those who do good without demanding recognition . <p> On a micro level , it 's the same principle we apply to random acts of kindness performed by strangers who expect no thanks in return . <p> Kindness costs nothing , and yet we all reap the benefits . Picture Shutterstock <p> Every so often , newspapers receive letters to the editor , or comments on social media pages , publicly thanking the unnamed and unidentified person who carried their groceries , helped them up after a fall on the street , paid for their coffee , helped jumpstart their car . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ little or nothing to perform , and increase the happiness of both the giver and receiver - sometimes by untold proportions . <p> It 's a mentality we should all aspire to - that kindness costs nothing , and yet we all reap the benefits . <p> Some acts of kindness do involve money - we 've all heard stories of strangers stepping in to pay for the groceries of a person clearly struggling to pay at the checkout , or mysterious benefactors contributing funds to help a struggling community get back on its feet . <p> Even those known for philanthropy often prefer that their donations remain anonymous , at least to the public . But there 's no requirement to do things anonymously - humility is the key attribute , here . <p> It 's a mentality we should all aspire to - that kindness costs nothing , and yet we all reap the benefits . <p> In protesting his lack of international recognition , Trump is showing us , ever more forcefully , that humility is not a colour in his palette . For some , it can be about karma - paying it forward , knowing implicitly that what goes around comes around . <p> For others , it 's simply a matter of doing what feels right at the time , without overthinking it . Paying for someone 's coffee without expecting thanks is simply a nice thing to do . <p> So is volunteering at the school fete , letting someone in front of you in the supermarket queue , mowing a neighbour 's lawn , or escorting a young person to their car after dark . <p> It 's ironic that it would take someone as hubristic as Donald Trump to remind us of the importance of random acts of kindness , but this is the world we 're living in.

This is n't a distant memory of a long-past conflict . It is happening right now . <p> For 30 million people in Sudan - more than Australia 's entire population - this horror is their daily life . Since early 2023 , Sudan has been consumed by a brutal civil war between the military and the paramilitary Rapid Support Forces ( RSF ) . <p> An estimated 150,000 people have been killed , and more than 12 million have been forced from their homes , making this the world 's largest displacement crisis . Famine conditions have been deliberately created through siege tactics . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of war . <p> In recent days , since the RSF overran El Fasher after an 18-month siege , at least 2000 civilians have been killed in what witnesses describe as systematic massacres . At one maternity hospital , 460 patients and their companions were executed . Satellite images show clusters of bodies in the streets . <p> UN officials are calling this Sudan 's Srebrenica moment . <p> So why is n't it dominating the news or trending on social media ? Why are there no mass protests ? Why no talk of the UN 's Blue Helmets ? <p> When Srebrenica happened in 1995 , the world was horrified - though it acted too late to save 8000 lives . When Rwanda 's genocide unfolded in 1994 , killing 800,000 people in 100 days , the world vowed " never again . Yet here we are , watching another mass atrocity unfold in slow motion and the silence is deafening . <p> In the wake of Gaza , has large-scale death and destruction become so common that it no longer moves us ? Have we become numb to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 're living through the largest number of armed conflicts since the Second World War . One in five children globally now lives in an active conflict zone . And as our recent Stop the War on Children report reveals , children in those conflicts are n't just caught in crossfire , they 're being deliberately targeted . In 2024 alone , grave violations against children in conflict jumped 30 per cent . Children are being killed , maimed , abducted , recruited as soldiers , and subjected to sexual violence in record numbers . <p> We 're not just seeing more wars . We 're seeing more brutal wars , where the rules that once protected civilians are being openly flouted while the accountability mechanisms meant to hold the perpetrators to account are being undermined and prevented from doing so . <p> We can not let this become normal . We can not become so accustomed to horror that we scroll past it . We can not allow mass atrocity to be just another news story competing for our attention between celebrity gossip and sports news . It is a massacre . To world leaders and humanitarian actors , I say this : laying siege to cities , starving communities , and killing civilians without accountability are crimes against our shared conscience . The people of Sudan deserve safety , dignity , and peace . Civilians need safe passage and unhindered humanitarian access now . Time has run out . They can not wait any longer . " <p> I know it can feel overwhelming , even hopeless . A safer world for them is a safer world for us all . <p> Aram Hosie is the director strategic communications and engagement at Save the Children Australia .

" <p> The legislation was drafted following extensive consultation with human rights advocates and Jewish , Islamic and other community groups . <p> The Human Rights Law Centre had raised concerns about the legal validity of a face mask ban , arguing it would criminalise people who wear them for health or cultural reasons , to maintain their privacy , or who wear mask coverings as a form of political communication or art . <p> The centre 's legal director , Sarah Schwartz , said on Monday that the High Court had affirmed that laws restricting peaceful protest had to be justified and proportionate . <p> " A blanket ban on face coverings at protests would not only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's implied freedom of political communication , " she said . @@131343839 <h> Interview : The Beldham star Katie Parker and director Angela Gulner on navigating emotional truth in the guise of a horror film <p> Struggling new mother Harper ( Katie Parker ) moves in with her own mother to renovate a fading suburban farmhouse . The two are joined by her mother 's new boyfriend and a mysterious live-in home aide , both who claim to be dedicated to Harper 's postpartum care . <p> But a sinister presence lurks within the house ... one that has designs on Harper 's child . <p> As Harper investigates , she uncovers dark truths about the nature of her reality , and questions the intentions of those entrusted with her care . <p> A multi-generational love story disguised as a horror , The Beldham explores the fine line between care and control , and the desperate lengths mothers will go to to protect the daughters they love . <p> As the film arrives on digital platforms in the United States ( you can read our review here ) , Peter Gray spoke with actress Parker and The Beldham 's director , Angela @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and what they hope audiences will take away from such a deep , dark , personal story . <p> Before I get to The Beldham , which is so much more than a horror film , but it has horror inclinations , absolutely . For both of you , was there a horror film that was your gateway into the genre ? <p> Katie Parker : I 'm not a horror movie person . I have a very delicate nervous system . I 'm a very nervous person . I get really gripped by watching something . I 'm one of those people that 's immediately in it , and then I ca n't let it go . I really avoided horror , and it was n't until I moved to Los Angeles and was trying to be an actor and I just happened to meet Mike Flanagan , who became a very close friend . He 's obviously the horror king , so that 's how I started . But I had never really seen a horror ( movie ) . I saw The Sixth Sense , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ slasher stuff . It 's really been in my 30s that I started to watch more , because I 'm more curious about how they do the twists . <p> Angela Gulner : Mine was definitely The Sixth Sense . That just has one of the best twists ever . It knocked my socks off . And I 'm a big Shyamalan fan , which is probably why this film has a twist to it . <p> Angela , you 've described the film as being born from watching the three generations of women navigate care and decline . How did you transform that cycle of love and loss into something cinematic ? Not just thematically , but visually and emotionally as well ? <p> Angela Gulner : It 's a good question . This might have some spoilers , but with my grandmother , moving her out of the house and then into assisted living , it was this slow move forward . I read a lot of books , and it kind of surprised me how much went into the film once I started visiting her . I @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with friends who had gone through this experience with grandparents , and I was just reading a lot about the paranoia and the violence that can come out , and the way you speak can change when you 're experiencing dementia . The " monster " was one of the last pieces that came into fruition . It took me a handful of drafts to get through ( my ) emotional distance from the story and from the characters , and do enough fictionalization to say , " Okay , I 'm allowed to have fun with the horror . " I had to take my time to build the distance . Otherwise , it was just too painful to put these characters through this . <p> And Katie , your character Harper , her journey is both psychological and supernatural . She 's haunted by care , essentially . How did you ground that experience so that it did n't feel like just horror acting , but something emotionally truthful about motherhood and identity ? <p> Katie Parker : Such beautiful questions , Peter . Angela really helped me @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ script and the character , and I think something I can rely on in my skillset as an actor , is the big emotional well that I have . I 'm trying to not play in that area all the time , because I want to grow and have other skills , but I just felt really supported by Angela and the crew to make ( my own ) choices . And because we were shooting in the same location , the house kind of lent itself as another character in this . Our art department was incredible . Really , this space was one of those creative experiences where just being there ... it was all there . I find filmmaking very frustrating , because I ca n't control a lot , and I want to control everything , and just leaning on what was happening naturally and letting it reveal itself was really helpful . <p> Katie Parker as Harper in The Beldham ( Wicked Myth Films and Rebellium Films ) <p> You 've both described the film as a love story disguised as a horror film . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for exploring the limits and the costs of love ? <p> Angela Gulner : I mean , gosh , such a great question . Katie started in theatre , and I find horror to be so theatrical . You can do so much movement , and on a second watch if you pay attention to Katie 's body language , there 's such beautiful work in the way she 's holding her body . It 's really subtle . I think you get to explore these really expanded feelings without falling into melodrama or sentiment , and there 's a space for those films too , but fear is a really exciting emotion that can kind of feel close to grief or close to anger , and they 're all vibrating in this heightened space . I do n't know , I think maybe feeling one gives you freedom to allow yourself to feel another without having to directly have the conversation about the really hard thing right away . That 's how I experience it . <p> Katie Parker : I feel like in life we 're always playing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The truth of our universe is that it 's incredibly dark , but there are babies being born , and people falling in love , and people are laughing , and then there is a war in Gaza and there 's genocide happening . Not to bring it there , but that is the duality of being alive on planet Earth . I think horror is the most accurate genre of living in that space all the time . Certainly with this movie , it 's horrific and frustrating and violent , but at the centre there 's a root of love . <p> It really was a film that had so much more to it than its horror inclinations , and it 's clear this vision comes from such a personal space . This family legacy is of caregiving and loss . Did that intimacy on Angela 's part change how you , Katie , approached your performance or your collaboration ? <p> Katie Parker : Angela and I are good friends , and she actually wrote this part for me . It was good information to have in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that personal experience , so it 's such a gift to have a writer and director give you their personal take . I like asking directors who the character is , or who the character is to them , because they can usually map right back to their own personal story . But a lot of the emotional stuff came from my own lived experience . <p> And a lot of horror movies about motherhood focus on guilt or madness . This film reaches for reconciliation and understanding . What kind of emotional catharsis do you both want audiences to walk away with ? <p> Angela Gulner : I want caregivers to feel seen . You can get angry and frustrated , and sometimes you get ugly or mean , and it 's just because you 're human . I am hoping that people who have been through that experience can see themselves and not be a villain . Maybe a bit of compassion and understanding for the person in the centre of the storm . <p> Katie Parker : I think , for me , I want people to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a great relationship with your mother , that every part of you deserves a seat at the table to be seen , and that is the most authentic way to be a person , to allow all the parts for us to be seen . And especially women , I feel like we all get fixated on being a certain way , and the human experience is way more complicated than that . <p> The Beldham is now available On Digital and On Demand in the United States .

@@131348391 <h> From climate crisis to AI catastrophe : The future no one is ready for <p> While leaders fumble climate action , the unchecked rise of superintelligent AI could eclipse every threat we know , writes Mark Beeson . <p> WELL-MEANING friends frequently tell me not to be so pessimistic , especially about our collective future . What 's the point of writing alarmist articles about the environment , for example , when " ordinary " people can do next to nothing to influence how a global problem plays out ? <p> At a time when pointless , anachronistic conflicts kill tens of thousands in Ukraine and Sudan , and a genocidal slaughter proves difficult to stop in Gaza , it 's hard not to turn away in despair . Likewise , when the world 's most powerful countries and largest polluters fail to even attend COP30 in Brazil , we can be forgiven for not being confident about the prospects for much-needed cooperation at the international level . <p> The good news is that such familiar threats to our security may not be quite as bad as we thought @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's an even greater potential problem : the creation of a superintelligent form of artificial intelligence ( AI ) . As Yudkowsky and Soares , the authors of one of the scariest and possibly most important books ever written , put it , If Anyone Builds It , Everyone Dies . <p> At this point , I should confess that I 'm not qualified to comment on the technical aspects of this argument . Much the same could be said about my grasp of climate science , but -- the stupendous ignorance of the most powerful man in the world notwithstanding -- most informed observers do not doubt the causes and potential consequences of climate change . I 'm happy to take their word for it . <p> Climate change is happening more quickly than even some of the pessimists expected , but we are encouraged to believe that the worst effects could be avoided if either market forces make renewables too cheap to resist , or states cooperate to make sure fossil fuels are rapidly phased out . At least we know what we 're up against and that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ignore them . <p> Unfortunately , the same can not be said about " artificial superintelligence " ( ASI ) . One of the problems -- which also shapes responses to the climate crisis , of course -- is that some people are making colossal amounts of money from business as usual . It is no coincidence that some of the most wealthy and powerful political figures around U.S. President Donald Trump are the tech bros , some of whom harbour delusional views about the future and their place in it . <p> Luckily , not all of the leaders in the race to exploit the potential of ASI are quite as self-absorbed . Nobel laureate Geoffrey Hinton , the so-called Godfather of AI , left his job at Google to warn of the imminent , poorly understood dangers that may emanate from the increasingly rapid , lavishly funded development of ASI . Not only is the private sector pouring huge sums into this research , but so are states . Everyone seems obsessed with not being left behind and letting competitors reap financial or strategic rewards . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Yudkowsky and Soares , is an ' alignment problem ' , in which the preferences of a ' mature ' ASI are ' vanishingly unlikely to align with our own ' . In other words , not only will ASI be much smarter than us very soon , but there is no reason to suppose they will think as we do or consider our interests ahead of their own . <p> Another scientific luminary , James Lovelock of Gaia thesis fame , thinks that in the future the Earth will be populated by cyborgs -- inorganic life-forms that will be vastly more intelligent than us . We 'll be lucky if they keep us as pets . <p> Given humanity 's predilection for violence and apparent inability to cooperate at the speed and scale necessary to address problems like climate change , cynics might argue they could hardly be less intelligent than us . <p> The immediate existential danger , according to Yudkowsky and Soares , is not just that ASI will be smarter than us and develop its own alien preferences , but that it may be able to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its goals , none of which are likely to include looking after human beings . I 'm not sure what " escaping onto the internet " actually means , but it does n't sound good . <p> Do n't worry , though , ' the issue is not that AIs will desire to dominate us ; rather , it 's that we are made of atoms they could use for something else ' . Not entirely reassuring , either . Given such apparently imminent threats , Yudkowsky and Soares argue that AI research should simply stop immediately until we know precisely what we are doing and have a better understanding of the risks we are running . <p> Given that Trump sees technological development as a chance for personal enrichment and Russian President Vladimir Putin thinks it 's a potentially critical source of battlefield advantage , it is difficult to see that happening . But still , look on the bright side : at least we may not have to worry about climate change for much longer . <p> Mark Beeson is an adjunct professor at the University of Technology @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ International Politics at the University of Western Australia .

@@131420720 <h> Great power rivalry <p> Ukraine : Russian forces this week continued to make steady but significant territorial gains in Ukraine , pushing into the hard-fought eastern city of Pokrovsk and advancing in the southern Zaporizhzhia region . <p> Russia has spent more than a year trying to capture Pokrovsk , a logistics hub and transport gateway that would allow it to advance towards other Ukrainian-held cities in the Donetsk region . <p> Ukraine acknowledged on Tuesday that about 300 Russian soldiers had entered the city as thick fog gave cover from drones . Footage showed Russian soldiers entering the city on motorbikes and trucks along roads scattered with debris . A Ukrainian MP , Oleksiy Goncharenko , wrote on Telegram : " We are losing Pokrovsk . The Russians have broken into the city . " <p> In Zaporizhzhia , Ukrainian forces withdrew from five villages after intense fighting and Russian artillery bombardments . <p> Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said defending Pokrovsk and Zaporizhzhia had been " difficult , in part because of weather conditions that favour the attacks " . <p> Separately , German investigators have gathered evidence @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for underwater explosions in September 2022 that sabotaged the Nord Stream gas pipeline between Russia and Germany . <p> Germany has issued arrest warrants for at least six people , but the case has caused tensions among Ukraine 's European allies . <p> In Poland , which opposed the pipeline , a court ruled in October that a Ukrainian diver , Volodymyr Zhuravlev , should not be extradited . The court said the attack was not sabotage but a legitimate act conducted during wartime . The Polish prime minister , Donald Tusk , said on X : " The problem with Nord Stream 2 is not that it was blown up . The problem is that it was built . " Hungary , which has close ties to Moscow , accused Tusk of trying to " defend terrorists " . <p> In Italy , a court approved the extradition of a former Ukrainian military officer , Serhiy Kuznetsov , who was detained in August . years of war . <p> Hold tight - we 're checking permissions before loading more content <p> Residents attempted to dig trenches to direct the water from flooding their tents , as rain dripped through tears in tarpaulins and makeshift shelters . <p> The first rain of the season pounded down in intermittent bursts , soaking the scant belongings families have managed to save . <p> Strong winds can also topple tents and destroy families ' attempts to gather food and supplies as another bleak winter sets in . <p> Two weeks ago , Bassil Naggar bought a new tent from the black market for 2300 NIS ( tents and belongings were completely wrecked . <p> " Water puddles are inches high , and there is no proper drainage , " he said <p> Barefoot children splashed in puddles as women made tea outside under dark clouds . <p> According to the UN , Muwasi , which was largely undeveloped dunes before the Israeli military designated it a humanitarian zone early in the war , held up to 425,000 displaced Palestinians this past summer , the vast majority living in makeshift temporary tents . <p> The Israeli defence body in charge of humanitarian aid in the Gaza Strip has said it is allowing in winter materials , including blankets and heavy tarpaulins , but aid organisations warn the efforts are far from sufficient when temperatures plummet in the winter and the wind whips off the sea . <p> As dragging its feet . <p> The first stage of the ceasefire agreement that took effect on October 10 is nearing its end . <p> The next stage calls for the implementation of a governing body for Gaza and the deployment of an international stabilisation force . <p> It is not clear where either stands . <p> Israel 's military campaign against Gaza has killed 69,100 , including many women and children , cent of its population of roughly two million Palestinians .

It was a breakthrough variety in its era , because of high yield , high quality , and rust resistance . <p> It is important to celebrate and recognise great past achievements , such as the development and release of wheat varieties that played a critical role in the expansion and profitability of farming . The release of Gabo wheat 80 years ago was one of these great achievements and it proved popular across the nation for many years . <h> Subscribe now for unlimited access to all our agricultural news across the nation <p> or signup to continue reading <p> All articles from our website &amp; app <p> The digital version of This Week 's Paper <p> Our entire network <p> Honorary Associate Professor and retired wheat breeder Dr Lindsay O'Brien and Sydney University Emeritus Professor Dr Peter Sharp @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ variety that transformed the Australian wheat industry which documents this great milestone . They extensively used materials in the personal library of Dr O'Brien and University of Sydney archives , including the WL Waterhouse collection of papers at the State Library of NSW . <p> A handful of wheat varieties with a major significance to Australian growers in our forebears ' eras include Federation ( 1901 release ) , Bencubbin ( 1929 ) and Gabo ( 1945 ) . <p> Breeding technology today sees far more rapid development and release of new varieties , with their popularity lasting much shorter periods . Wheat breeders of early eras had far fewer resources and depended greatly on cooperating farmers , not just for use of their land but sowing , harvesting and assessing breeders ' trials . <p> Kate Lorimer-Ward , CEO of Local Land Service and former department director general of NSW DPI , presenting one of the authors of the Gabo book , Lindsay O'Brien , with the distinguished Farrer Medal . <p> As the book details , Gabo arrived at a time when the industry badly needed a rust-resistant @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and with good milling and baking quality . Adoption of Gabo was most striking in northern areas where rust resistance was at the front of grower 's needs . <p> By 1954 it was the most widely grown Australian variety . From 1950 to the mid-1960s Gabo remained a leading national variety . <p> Sydney University rust researcher and wheat breeder Walter Lawry Waterhouse developed Gabo . He was an academic who started rust studies at the University of Sydney in 1922 with the hope of producing rust-resistant varieties . Waterhouse obtained rust resistance for Gabo from the durum wheat Gaza , of Palestinian origin . <p> By todays standards he had rudimentary facilities at the University 's Camperdown campus . He had access to basic glasshouse space and a small area adjacent to it . He could make crosses in the glasshouse and screen plants for rust using seedling tests . <p> Vital to his breeding program was his former Hawkesbury Agricultural College classmate Charles Beeson , who settled on the land at Leyburn west of Gunnedah in 1908 . Beeson provided land , his time and his employees @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ breeding plots using his farm machinery . <p> Federation wheat variety , developed by William Farrer , and released in 1901 . The first of the famous varieties that played a major role in the development of the Australian wheat industry . <p> Gabo seed production was conducted by Claude Hathway on his property Woodleigh , south of Gunnedah . His father settled at Woodleigh in 1903 . Claude was also looking at different wheats for a solution to the rust problem and worked closely with Waterhouse . Hawkesbury Agricultural College in Richmond and NSW Department of Agriculture Cowra research centre were also used for screening . <p> Waterhouse also sent his best lines to breeders in other states and had feedback on them from scientists like Albert Pugsley , Waite Institute , Adelaide ; George Gordon , Werribee , Vic ; and EJ Breakwell , Roseworthy Agricultural College SA . He also sent lines to New Zealand and Kenya for stem rust testing and the USA when young researchers Watson and Macindoe took material with them for their doctoral studies . <p> Screening for quality was a big part @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ released , millers offered premiums to growers for higher protein parcels of Gabo . Growers banded together and formed the Premium Wheat Growers Association to ensure fair quality dividends . So began another big chapter in wheat production . <p> This extremely easy to read and interesting 151-page book is available from Secretary Gunnedah Rural Museum , PO Box 366 , Gunnedah , 2380 or by calling ( 02 ) 6742 4690 . Cost is $30 delivered .

@@131592801 <h> Latest posts <p> 4.01pm on Nov 19 , 2025 <h> Diabolic : If the Bard could do Danish tragedy , we can do American horror <h> By Jake Wilson <p> Diabolic ? ? ( MA ) 95 minutes <p> If Shakespeare could write plays set in Denmark and ancient Rome without anyone questioning their cultural relevance to the British public , I 'm not about to insist Australian films need be overtly Australian . <p> Elizabeth Cullen in Diabolic . <p> Still , pretending we 're on the other side of the world does add a degree of difficulty , which may account for some of the limitations of Daniel J. Phillips ' horror film Diabolic , set in Utah but shot in South Australia . <p> The accents of the Aussie leads pass muster as far as I can judge , and Phillips and his team have been careful about avoiding gum trees and ensuring steering wheels are on the left . Harder to gauge is how accurately they have portrayed the secretive Fundamentalist Church of Latter-Day Saints , a real-life breakaway Mormon sect sometimes described as a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ FLDS in her teens , Elise ( Elizabeth Cullen ) is striving to live a normal life with her boyfriend , Adam ( John Kim ) , but suffers from fits of insanity during which she 's capable of digging holes all over the backyard or slaughtering the neighbours ' pets . <p> The answer , according to her therapist , is to confront her half-forgotten but evidently traumatic past . Reckless even by the standards of movie therapists , he hooks her up with a mother-son team of psychedelic healers ( Genevieve Mooy and Dennis Coard ) aligned with the FLDS , who operate out of a cabin deep in the Utah woods and speak in a lilting manner that sounds almost but not quite Irish ( a dialect coach is credited ) . <p> Under the supervision of this dubious pair , Elise and Adam enter what is referred to as the " dream state " , making Diabolic the fourth or possibly fifth film I 've reviewed in the past month in which characters take a psychedelic trip of some kind , in contexts ranging from comedy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ documentary ( the locally made Edge of Life ) . <p> Maybe this reflects our changing world , or maybe it 's just caught on as a device allowing filmmakers to cut to the chase . Phillips does n't go all in on trippy imagery , but the drug does give Elise a chance to relive repressed memories , filling in some of the missing pieces of her backstory . <p> Other ambiguities linger , not always effectively . It 's not wholly clear if we 're meant to see the FLDS purely as a repressive , evil institution , or as fundamentally correct , despite everything , about how reality works . <p> Nor is it clear what tone Phillips is aiming for . The style is muted and moody in " elevated horror " fashion , but the dialogue is on the flatly literal side , and there 's a tendency to rush through major plot developments to get to the jump scares . <p> On the whole , the film works best the nearer it comes to being a character study : Cullen 's hushed , abrupt @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , damaged characters ( as does her hair , unkempt fringe and all ) . ( M ) 112 minutes <p> Perhaps the greatest strengths of Iranian director Sepideh Farsi 's heartbreaking documentary about life under siege in Gaza lie in the film 's flaws . <p> It invites us to sit in on a series of conversations between Farsi and Fatma Hassona , a 25 year-old Palestinian woman who manages to keep smiling through all the hazards and privations she experiences . The internet connection is highly temperamental . Their talks are frequently disrupted by power cuts , buffering and Fatma 's need to move whenever Israeli shells hit a nearby street . Yet @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , shifting from her family 's apartment to a neighbour 's house whenever the line is down . <p> And in between their conversations , she writes poetry and goes outside to photograph the wreckage in which she and her family exist . And still the smile persists . <p> Farsi says that she sees Fatma , who is the same age as her daughter , as a mirror image of her teenage self . She was 18 when she escaped Iran after being arrested and imprisoned two years earlier as a dissident . She 's been based in Paris ever since , making documentaries , dramas and an animation , while continuing to speak out against the Iranian regime . <p> In 2024 , after trying to get into Gaza , she was filming Palestinian refugees in Cairo when she heard about Fatma and the photographs she was posting online . By the end of April , their phone calls had begun . <p> The documentary invites us to sit in on a series of conversations.Credit : Via Hi Gloss Entertainment <p> In one early conversation , Fatma recalls @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sound of Apache helicopters to see white lines striping the sky . Unable to understand what was happening , she had no hint of the carnage to come . Nor did she foresee the fact that food and how to get it was about to become an overriding preoccupation . Chicken , chocolate and Nescafe feature prominently in her daydreams , she tells Farsi . " I miss everything . " <p> By the end of June , the Israeli army is in the area , the bombing has intensified and the family is told to evacuate to the west . Fatma takes her camera but everything else is left behind in the house and they walk . It sounds like a hair-raising journey but they have now arrived back home and they 're looking for food and clean water , with the sound of the bombing getting closer . Managing a nervous laugh , she says : " I 'm shaking . " <p> We meet her brothers , who are curious about Farsi , one of the few foreign women they have met , but their lack of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ And Fatma 's smile fades when she talks of the friends she has lost . Farsi has heard that the film has been accepted for the independent film section of the Cannes festival and the conversation ends as the women dare to raise the possibility that Fatma might get permission to be there . ( M ) 132 minutes <p> Is George Clooney a movie star , or just an actor who specialises in playing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has outwardly been in the sweet spot of middle age for the past three decades -- both movie star qualities . But there are fewer hits than you might expect -- or at least , fewer movies that became hits because the public was drawn to Clooney in particular . <p> Jay Kelly , on the other hand , is definitely a movie star . But Noah Baumbach 's new comedy-drama Jay Kelly is not a portrait of Clooney , although Clooney slips smoothly into the role of Jay , and whatever charisma Jay possesses is Clooney 's first . <p> Adam Sandler ( left ) plays George Clooney 's loyal manager , Ron . <p> Jay too has been around for decades , and has done it all , without losing his credibility or cool . His pampered life outwardly leaves him with little to worry about , not that he was ever the fretful type . <p> Still , there comes a time when you have to ask yourself what it 's all amounted to , especially given the sacrifices he 's made to stay at the top @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the time away from his kids . <p> Baumbach co-wrote the screenplay for Barbie , directed by his wife , Greta Gerwig , and like Barbie , this film can be viewed as a variant on the Pinocchio theme : the puppet yearning to become a real boy . <p> Jay 's mock-heroic quest takes him from LA to Paris , where he reunites with his younger daughter Daisy ( Grace Edwards ) , and then on to Tuscany , where he 's due to receive a special tribute teed up by loyal manager Ron ( Adam Sandler ) . This cues a series of flashbacks covering different aspects of his past , in which he 's sometimes played by young British actor Charlie Rowe . <p> It sounds potentially corny , and also like an uphill battle to keep us interested in the internal struggles of this extremely fortunate man . The movie keeps threatening to turn sentimental and finally does : with a bit of rejigging , the final sequence could have been left out altogether , which would have been a good idea . <p> Still , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's co-writer Emily Mortimer between them offer a partially satirical , mostly clear-eyed take on their hero : his sunny self-involvement , his genuine but also possibly limited talent , and the way his celebrity operates as a force field affecting everyone around him . <p> Much of the time this is less a film about Jay than about how the supporting characters react to the Jay-shaped space at the story 's centre . It 's also about the nature of acting , generously allowing one cast member after another a chance in the spotlight -- with Sandler , in his heyday a bigger star than Clooney ever was , coming closest to stealing the show . <p> The nudging self-awareness peaks with the casting of Billy Crudup as a gifted acting school classmate of Jay 's who dropped out of the industry ( he 's become a " child therapist " , a typical Baumbach gag ) . In reality , Crudup never quite achieved the stardom that once looked like his for the taking , though it 's easier to imagine him playing Clooney 's role here than @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ career has lasted almost as long as Clooney 's , he might be wondering if he in turn could be described as a " hero of cinema " , a label pinned on Jay by a fan . Not quite , in my eyes . But a man who can envisage Sandler and Laura Dern as a couple who never were has at least occasional flashes of genius .

year , the day after the Cannes Film Festival included the work-in-progress documentary in its 2025 program . <p> In this TikTok generation of quick news bites , it 's impossible to watch this @@131631951 <p> The Turkish tea queue was double that of the Australian coffee line on Thursday morning in Bel ? m , Brazil , host of the COP30 UN climate talks . <p> Next door , in the Australian pavilion , a clutch of staffers and departmental reps milled around chatting in the cold light of day . <p> The news , of course , broke the night before : Australia would not be host to COP31 . Instead , tens of thousands of delegates and their camp followers -- civil society and the fossil lobby included -- would travel to the Turkish resort town of Antalya in 2026 for its " lower carbon " climate COP . <p> Negotiations were still ongoing -- until they were n't . That was the line to the bitter end . For days , it had been used to keep civil society groups on the ground in check , and to brush off media enquiries from the only two Australian reporters present . <p> Departmental reps and ministerial advisers faithfully repeated the phrase in response to every enquiry , every request for clarity , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sources piled up and the Turkish delegation were overheard celebrating . <p> " I am informed negotiations are ongoing . " There was nothing more to say , apparently . No clarification or explanation about what exactly was being negotiated . Negotiations were happening , and they were ongoing . That was it . That 's all anyone needed to know . <p> When an answer did come , it was swift : a clipped , five minute doorstop delivered by climate change and energy minister Chris Bowen outside the Australian delegation office . <p> Its thrust was predictable : state Australia 's goals , glance furtively in the general direction of Russia without naming names when talking about vetoes , frame the defeat as a tactical retreat in defence of multilateralism , unpack some of the detail and offer condolences . <p> " I know some people will be disappointed in that outcome . Other people , of course , would be even more disappointed if it had gone to Bonn without a COP president in place , " Bowen said . <p> This last bit was misleading . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ remained the COP President -- but that 's a technicality . If anyone was disappointed in the outcome , it would have been Bowen , who had championed the bid , as had his department . <p> Staff had been seconded , plans formed . COP31 could have been the first ever climate negotiation held in Oceania , a bold chance for Australia , a major fossil fuel producer , to weigh in on international affairs and to show , for once and for all , it was serious about climate change . <p> It was not to be -- an outcome that might be welcomed in some quarters . Since the failed Indigenous Voice to Parliament referendum , Australia is n't in the business of being bold . It does business as usual . Boldness , after all , attracts attention . Boldness is risky and does n't carry the same return on investment as gas or coal . <p> In recent weeks , the Coalition , a historical close friend of the country 's oil , gas and coal producers , finally decided to come clean about its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it thinks it 's bunk . <p> Hosting a COP in Adelaide next November would have guaranteed 12 months of headlines from the Coalition whinging about how much it will cost and how all it will do is give away taxpayer dollars to developing countries . <p> It would be cheaper for everyone to stay home -- as Albanese had . According to Labor sources , the PM had a lot on during that first week of COP30 , anyway . <p> If the domestic politics around climate change were unclear , it was DCCEEW departmental secretary Mike Kaiser who set things straight on Monday in Bel ? m when he said the government could only move as fast as median voters Suburban Joe and Country Jane allowed . <p> " Whether it 's fossil fuel subsidies or any other topic that we might wish , from a public sector perspective that government takes more rapid action on , you know , they 've got an interest in being returned to office , " Kaiser said . <p> " And if it was so blindingly obvious that that was a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ path . These issues , when seen from the perspective of a national government that has to balance considerations , are always more complex . <p> " If it was blindingly obvious , if it was blindingly beneficial to a government , politically , it would do it tomorrow . Common sense makes sense . " <p> Of course , those average Australians who lost their homes when the East Coast burned during the Black Christmas bushfires and Lismore drowned may disagree . <p> In the bigger picture , the result adds to a string of tactical retreats for the small-target Albanese government and its large majority in the lower house . Prior to the election , the Prime Minister intervened at the last minute to yank a deal with The Greens on environmental reforms negotiated by Tanya Plibersek . <p> Ed Husic was dumped from Cabinet and moved to the backbench as Israel 's siege of Gaza reached a crescendo . Even federal Treasurer Jim Chalmers backed down on superannuation reform . <p> Whether or not any conclusions might be drawn from pattern of behaviour , the reaction to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up a storm about the " Pacific Family " , but did not deliver . Woodside , meanwhile , seems to get whatever it wants . <p> All the financial and political capital civil society , industry and Indigenous groups had invested into the bid over two years turned out to be for nothing . In the cold light of day , some tried to find a silver lining in a negotiated solution that tried to ensure the Pacific won something out of COP31 . <p> Others quietly -- and strictly off the record -- felt they had been played for fools . Despite the efforts to frame the loss as a valiant defeat following a noble effort , many walked away with a feeling domestic Australian politics had trumped the public good . <p> Now that COP30 in Brazil is no longer about Australia , many Australians may switch off as the process lurches on . Ten years after the Paris Agreement was signed , even as emissions continue to rise , the final texts are yet to be negotiated and agreed . <p> What is decided in Bel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , responds as the atmosphere continues to destabilise . <p> Billions in investment will be re-routed based on these words , and the commitments governments make on issues like the Just Transition Mechanism and a possible fossil fuel roadmap will determine the fate of oil , gas and coal . <p> As of Thursday in Bel ? m , negotiations do in fact remain ongoing -- at least until a fire in the Blue Zone forced an evacuation , and a delay . <p> When you login first time using a Social Login button , we collect your account public profile information shared by Social Login provider , based on your privacy settings . @@131653514 <p> Earlier this month , footage of about 60 black-clad men outside the New South Wales Parliament sent a chill through many Australians , certainly those with a sense of history . <p> The demonstrators had an anti-Semitic banner ( Abolish the Jewish Lobby ) and chanted the Hitler Youth slogan " blood and honour " . <p> Among officialdom , the rally has had a galvanising effect . The federal government this week took one of the demonstrators , South African Matthew Gruter , into immigration detention , readying to deport him . <p> Home Affairs Minister Tony Burke says he is having continuous conversations with agencies to ensure federal laws are adequate to deal with the neo-Nazis . <p> The NSW Labor government introduced legislation " to ban conduct which indicates support for Nazi ideology by invoking imagery or characteristics associated with Nazism " . NSW Attorney-General Michael Daley said in a statement that while the state already banned Nazi symbols , " the disgraceful rally outside the Parliament ... highlighted the need to strengthen current laws " . <p> The move is controversial . NSW Council for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do not fight fascists with laws that erode civil liberties " . <p> In a speech early this month , ASIO boss Mike Burgess highlighted the threat , and challenge , the neo-Nazis are posing . <p> Burgess said that currently there were " multiple , cascading and intersecting threats " to Australia 's social cohesion . These were " fuelled by three distinct but connected cohorts : the aggrieved , the opportunistic , and the cunning " . <p> He put the neo-Nazis into the " opportunistic " category , pointing to how they had attempted to leverage the anti-immigration marches . <p> " The biggest neo-Nazi group , the National Socialist Network - or White Australia as it is rebranding itself - identified the demonstrations as a vehicle to raise its profile , " he said . <p> " It strategically and opportunistically exploited the organisers ' complaints about immigration and the cost of living . <p> " This is a key part of the network 's broader strategy to ' mainstream ' and expand its movement by focusing on issues with broader appeal . " <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ raising issues for Australia 's democracy that are out of proportion to their small numbers . Combating them is complicated , because it tests the interface between societal harm and political rights . <p> The neo-Nazis are now canvassing the prospect of registering a political party and running candidates for parliament . To register in NSW would require just 750 members , or 1500 to become a party federally . <p> If the neo-Nazis did get party status it is not impossible they could have a candidate elected to the NSW upper house , or even - a long shot , admittedly - to the Senate . <p> Assuming the neo-Nazis meet the criteria , any attempt to stop them registering a party would run up against the constitution 's implied right of political communication . As would an attempt to ban them . <p> Peter Wertheim , co-CEO of the Executive Council of Australian Jewry , says the prospect of neo-Nazis in parliament " spewing their hate under the protection of parliamentary privilege is intolerable . It would put our country on the slippery slope to normalising racism " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ under the International Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination " to declare illegal and prohibit organisations which promote and incite racial discrimination " . <p> But , he says , " any legislative action would need to be framed as a means of regulating or proscribing clearly-defined conduct , rather than specific persons and bodies " . <p> Rosalind Dixon , professor of law at UNSW , writing in the Sydney Morning Herald this week , argued the electoral registration laws should be revisited to give federal and state electoral commissions " express power to ban parties that espouse racial hate and vilification as founding policies and values " , or else do so through a demonstrated pattern of conduct . <p> That would likely run into opposition from the electoral commissions . The Australian Electoral Commission is on record as strongly opposed to proposals it should be a watchdog on truth in political advertising . <p> But Dixon suggests that such powers could be subject to robust judicial oversight , or possibly even given to courts under well-defined legislative criteria . This , she says @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and ensure that the power was subject to appropriate limits and oversight , necessary to meet constitutional requirements . <p> Liberal frontbencher Julian Leeser , prominent on Jewish issues , says parliament 's Joint Standing Committee on Electoral Matters should be asked to look at whether it is possible to restrict the neo-Nazis ' political participation . Leeser believes , on the basis of previous High Court cases , that a legal counterweight could be found to the right to political communications . <p> While the case for banning terrorist groups is obvious , trying to outlaw groups like the neo-Nazis , even if possible , is problematic , however attractive it sounds in theory . <p> Leaving aside arguments about free speech and association , driving these people underground could be counterproductive , helping them attract supporters and making them harder to track . <p> The neo-Nazis exploit feelings of alienation among a section of the community . Making martyrs of them could play into their hands . <p> State and federal laws to deal with hate speech have been strengthened as governments have grappled with threats to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in October 2023 , the ensuing Gaza war , pro-Palestinian demonstrations and encampments , and anti-Semitic attacks . <p> These events have triggered a torrent of hate speech , particularly from the extremists elements among the pro-Palestinian activists . <p> Trying to stop hate speech turns into an ever-escalating pursuit . While action is desirable , at some point it runs into pushback on grounds of free speech . <p> Finding ways to curb the presence of extremist groups such as , but not only , the neo-Nazis falls into the category political scientists call " wicked problems " to which there are no simple , direct answers . <p> It 's understandable to turn to new or strengthened legal remedies and , to an extent , appropriate . But it can only be part of the answer and often comes with unintended or unacceptable consequences . <p> In the end , these groups may be best countered more indirectly , by vigorously promoting positive measures that advance social cohesion in the community . But unfortunately that 's a long term answer to an immediate problem . <p> Michelle Grattan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Canberra Times . @@131663558 <h> Eurovision changes rules after Israel controversy <p> Organisers of the Eurovision Song Contest have announced changes to voting rules to avoid state interference after controversy over Israel 's entry this year . <p> Hold tight - we 're checking permissions before loading more content <p> The new rules by the European Broadcasting Union discourage governments and third parties from " disproportionately promoting " songs to sway voters -
